People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. But my mind does keep circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd then there’s this… concern that once I’m fully retired, I’ll become sort of useless. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, with it suddenly peaking within a few minutes. I get worked up, certainly, but it’s more of a steady worrying than an abrupt wave of fear. At least, that’s how I’d put it—if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty most of the time… no, I don’t think I’d put it that way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no… at least nothing I’d call traumatic. I’ve had stressful periods, of course—work and life and decisions piling up—but I don’t think there’s one particular event that stands out in that way.\n\nI hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more general and about everything in life?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything. It’s more that I slow things down. With the retirement handoff, I keep second-guessing decisions and then postponing parts of it, telling myself I just need to review things once more.

And at the clinic, I can lose time going back over things, which makes the day feel less manageable. Even the things I usually enjoy, like painting or gardening, I’ll put off because they feel like more than I can quite take on at the moment. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance, but… yes, probably some.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think there is an urge, though I’d hesitate to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more like I feel I can’t quite settle until I’ve done something about the thought.

I’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and that helps briefly. But then one concern leads to the next, and I end up checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same question more than once. I know that probably isn’t necessary, but in the moment it feels hard to just leave it alone. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

And then I start wondering what I’m going to be worth, I suppose, once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know some of that is normal to think about, but my mind does tend to circle around it… probably more than it needs to. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything. It’s more that I slow things down. With the retirement handoff, I keep second-guessing decisions and then postponing parts of it, telling myself I just need to review things once more.\n\nAnd at the clinic, I can lose time going back over things, which makes the day feel less manageable. Even the things I usually enjoy, like painting or gardening, I’ll put off because they feel like more than I can quite take on at the moment. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance, but… yes, probably some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts over and over?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… not in a ritual sort of way, if that’s what you mean. More that I’ll try to talk myself through it logically, the way I would with a nervous patient, and for a moment it helps.\n\nBut then one concern seems to lead straight into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same questions more than once. I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I suppose… but I do have trouble letting it go. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to perform these repetitive behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there is an urge, though I’d hesitate to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more like I feel I can’t quite settle until I’ve done something about the thought.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and that helps briefly. But then one concern leads to the next, and I end up checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same question more than once. I know that probably isn’t necessary, but in the moment it feels hard to just leave it alone. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I’m not thinking something terrible will happen in a very specific sense, but I do feel like if I don’t address the worry, I’m being careless.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and that helps for a bit. But then one concern leads to another, and I end up checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question again when I probably don’t need to. I tell myself I’m just being responsible, but… I think I’m also trying to stop something from going wrong, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I mean, I have my moments, like anyone, and I can get discouraged with myself, but I wouldn’t say it’s a constant sadness. At least, not most of the time. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no… at least nothing I’d call traumatic. I’ve had stressful periods, of course—work and life and decisions piling up—but I don’t think there’s one particular event that stands out in that way.\n\nI hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more general and about everything in life?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, there are a few themes that come up a lot—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing. And then I start wondering what I’m going to be worth, I suppose, once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know some of that is normal to think about, but my mind does tend to circle around it… probably more than it needs to. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything. It’s more that I slow things down. With the retirement handoff, I keep second-guessing decisions and then postponing parts of it, telling myself I just need to review things once more.\n\nAnd at the clinic, I can lose time going back over things, which makes the day feel less manageable. Even the things I usually enjoy, like painting or gardening, I’ll put off because they feel like more than I can quite take on at the moment. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance, but… yes, probably some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts over and over?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… not in a ritual sort of way, if that’s what you mean. More that I’ll try to talk myself through it logically, the way I would with a nervous patient, and for a moment it helps.\n\nBut then one concern seems to lead straight into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same questions more than once. I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I suppose… but I do have trouble letting it go. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to perform these repetitive behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there is an urge, though I’d hesitate to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more like I feel I can’t quite settle until I’ve done something about the thought.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and that helps briefly. But then one concern leads to the next, and I end up checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same question more than once. I know that probably isn’t necessary, but in the moment it feels hard to just leave it alone. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I’m not thinking something terrible will happen in a very specific sense, but I do feel like if I don’t address the worry, I’m being careless.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and that helps for a bit. But then one concern leads to another, and I end up checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question again when I probably don’t need to. I tell myself I’m just being responsible, but… I think I’m also trying to stop something from going wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like repeating these behaviors or mental acts helps to reduce your anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… briefly, I think it does. I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m not trying to be difficult or anything, but when I check the email again or go back over my notes, I do feel a little calmer for a moment.\n\nThe problem is it doesn’t seem to last very long. I’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and I can almost settle it… but then another concern comes up, and then I’m reviewing something again or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go. So yes, it reduces the worry a bit, but not in a way that really holds.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no… at least not that I can name clearly. It’s more that I feel uneasy if I don’t follow the thought through.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, the way I might with a nervous patient, and sometimes that helps for a minute. But then another concern pops up, and I’m back checking an email, or looking over notes again, or asking the same question when I probably already know the answer.\n\nSo I suppose I’m trying to prevent mistakes in a general sense, but it’s not usually one exact disaster I’m picturing. Is that what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

And then I start wondering what I’m going to be worth, I suppose, once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know some of that is normal to think about, but my mind does tend to circle around it… probably more than it needs to. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything. It’s more that I slow things down. With the retirement handoff, I keep second-guessing decisions and then postponing parts of it, telling myself I just need to review things once more.\n\nAnd at the clinic, I can lose time going back over things, which makes the day feel less manageable. Even the things I usually enjoy, like painting or gardening, I’ll put off because they feel like more than I can quite take on at the moment. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance, but… yes, probably some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts over and over?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… not in a ritual sort of way, if that’s what you mean. More that I’ll try to talk myself through it logically, the way I would with a nervous patient, and for a moment it helps.\n\nBut then one concern seems to lead straight into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same questions more than once. I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I suppose… but I do have trouble letting it go. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to perform these repetitive behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there is an urge, though I’d hesitate to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more like I feel I can’t quite settle until I’ve done something about the thought.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and that helps briefly. But then one concern leads to the next, and I end up checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same question more than once. I know that probably isn’t necessary, but in the moment it feels hard to just leave it alone. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I’m not thinking something terrible will happen in a very specific sense, but I do feel like if I don’t address the worry, I’m being careless.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and that helps for a bit. But then one concern leads to another, and I end up checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question again when I probably don’t need to. I tell myself I’m just being responsible, but… I think I’m also trying to stop something from going wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like repeating these behaviors or mental acts helps to reduce your anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… briefly, I think it does. I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m not trying to be difficult or anything, but when I check the email again or go back over my notes, I do feel a little calmer for a moment.\n\nThe problem is it doesn’t seem to last very long. I’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and I can almost settle it… but then another concern comes up, and then I’m reviewing something again or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go. So yes, it reduces the worry a bit, but not in a way that really holds.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no… at least not that I can name clearly. It’s more that I feel uneasy if I don’t follow the thought through.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, the way I might with a nervous patient, and sometimes that helps for a minute. But then another concern pops up, and I’m back checking an email, or looking over notes again, or asking the same question when I probably already know the answer.\n\nSo I suppose I’m trying to prevent mistakes in a general sense, but it’s not usually one exact disaster I’m picturing. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like making a mistake is one of the main things that's driving your need to repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m incapable or anything, but making a mistake is definitely part of what gets me caught up.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, almost the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked it or that it’s probably fine. But then one concern leads into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose the fear of making a mistake is a big part of it… if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean, I have my moments, like anyone, and I can get discouraged with myself, but I wouldn’t say it’s a constant sadness. At least, not most of the time. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no… at least nothing I’d call traumatic. I’ve had stressful periods, of course—work and life and decisions piling up—but I don’t think there’s one particular event that stands out in that way.\n\nI hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more general and about everything in life?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, there are a few themes that come up a lot—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing. And then I start wondering what I’m going to be worth, I suppose, once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know some of that is normal to think about, but my mind does tend to circle around it… probably more than it needs to. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything. It’s more that I slow things down. With the retirement handoff, I keep second-guessing decisions and then postponing parts of it, telling myself I just need to review things once more.\n\nAnd at the clinic, I can lose time going back over things, which makes the day feel less manageable. Even the things I usually enjoy, like painting or gardening, I’ll put off because they feel like more than I can quite take on at the moment. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance, but… yes, probably some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts over and over?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… not in a ritual sort of way, if that’s what you mean. More that I’ll try to talk myself through it logically, the way I would with a nervous patient, and for a moment it helps.\n\nBut then one concern seems to lead straight into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same questions more than once. I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I suppose… but I do have trouble letting it go. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to perform these repetitive behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there is an urge, though I’d hesitate to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more like I feel I can’t quite settle until I’ve done something about the thought.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and that helps briefly. But then one concern leads to the next, and I end up checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same question more than once. I know that probably isn’t necessary, but in the moment it feels hard to just leave it alone. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I’m not thinking something terrible will happen in a very specific sense, but I do feel like if I don’t address the worry, I’m being careless.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and that helps for a bit. But then one concern leads to another, and I end up checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question again when I probably don’t need to. I tell myself I’m just being responsible, but… I think I’m also trying to stop something from going wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like repeating these behaviors or mental acts helps to reduce your anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… briefly, I think it does. I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m not trying to be difficult or anything, but when I check the email again or go back over my notes, I do feel a little calmer for a moment.\n\nThe problem is it doesn’t seem to last very long. I’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and I can almost settle it… but then another concern comes up, and then I’m reviewing something again or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go. So yes, it reduces the worry a bit, but not in a way that really holds.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no… at least not that I can name clearly. It’s more that I feel uneasy if I don’t follow the thought through.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, the way I might with a nervous patient, and sometimes that helps for a minute. But then another concern pops up, and I’m back checking an email, or looking over notes again, or asking the same question when I probably already know the answer.\n\nSo I suppose I’m trying to prevent mistakes in a general sense, but it’s not usually one exact disaster I’m picturing. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like making a mistake is one of the main things that's driving your need to repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m incapable or anything, but making a mistake is definitely part of what gets me caught up.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, almost the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked it or that it’s probably fine. But then one concern leads into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose the fear of making a mistake is a big part of it… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or reviews primarily focused on preventing something from going wrong in a specific area of your life?\n\nPatient: Not one specific area, I don’t think. I mean… work is where it probably shows up the most, because there are emails and notes and things that feel important to get right. But it’s not like I’m only trying to prevent one particular thing from going wrong.\n\nIt’s more that once I start feeling unsure, I try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already handled it. But then one concern leads to the next, and I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo… I’d say it’s more general than specific, if that answers it properly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally made me make the appointment is… my concentration has gotten a bit unreliable. I mean, I can still do my work, of course, but I’ll be reviewing a chart at the clinic, or even doing something I enjoy like laying out a watercolor wash, and then I realize I’ve read the same line three times. My mind has wandered off to all the things that could go wrong. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you mean, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about the future or things that could go wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because some worry is normal, I know. But my mind does keep circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd then there’s this… concern that once I’m fully retired, I’ll become sort of useless. It feels a bit out of proportion even to me, honestly. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, with it suddenly peaking within a few minutes. I get worked up, certainly, but it’s more of a steady worrying than an abrupt wave of fear. At least, that’s how I’d put it—if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty most of the time… no, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I mean, I have my moments, like anyone, and I can get discouraged with myself, but I wouldn’t say it’s a constant sadness. At least, not most of the time. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no… at least nothing I’d call traumatic. I’ve had stressful periods, of course—work and life and decisions piling up—but I don’t think there’s one particular event that stands out in that way.\n\nI hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more general and about everything in life?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, there are a few themes that come up a lot—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing. And then I start wondering what I’m going to be worth, I suppose, once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know some of that is normal to think about, but my mind does tend to circle around it… probably more than it needs to. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything. It’s more that I slow things down. With the retirement handoff, I keep second-guessing decisions and then postponing parts of it, telling myself I just need to review things once more.\n\nAnd at the clinic, I can lose time going back over things, which makes the day feel less manageable. Even the things I usually enjoy, like painting or gardening, I’ll put off because they feel like more than I can quite take on at the moment. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance, but… yes, probably some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts over and over?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… not in a ritual sort of way, if that’s what you mean. More that I’ll try to talk myself through it logically, the way I would with a nervous patient, and for a moment it helps.\n\nBut then one concern seems to lead straight into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same questions more than once. I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I suppose… but I do have trouble letting it go. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to perform these repetitive behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there is an urge, though I’d hesitate to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more like I feel I can’t quite settle until I’ve done something about the thought.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and that helps briefly. But then one concern leads to the next, and I end up checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same question more than once. I know that probably isn’t necessary, but in the moment it feels hard to just leave it alone. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I’m not thinking something terrible will happen in a very specific sense, but I do feel like if I don’t address the worry, I’m being careless.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and that helps for a bit. But then one concern leads to another, and I end up checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question again when I probably don’t need to. I tell myself I’m just being responsible, but… I think I’m also trying to stop something from going wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like repeating these behaviors or mental acts helps to reduce your anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… briefly, I think it does. I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m not trying to be difficult or anything, but when I check the email again or go back over my notes, I do feel a little calmer for a moment.\n\nThe problem is it doesn’t seem to last very long. I’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and I can almost settle it… but then another concern comes up, and then I’m reviewing something again or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go. So yes, it reduces the worry a bit, but not in a way that really holds.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no… at least not that I can name clearly. It’s more that I feel uneasy if I don’t follow the thought through.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, the way I might with a nervous patient, and sometimes that helps for a minute. But then another concern pops up, and I’m back checking an email, or looking over notes again, or asking the same question when I probably already know the answer.\n\nSo I suppose I’m trying to prevent mistakes in a general sense, but it’s not usually one exact disaster I’m picturing. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like making a mistake is one of the main things that's driving your need to repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m incapable or anything, but making a mistake is definitely part of what gets me caught up.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, almost the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked it or that it’s probably fine. But then one concern leads into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose the fear of making a mistake is a big part of it… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or reviews primarily focused on preventing something from going wrong in a specific area of your life?\n\nPatient: Not one specific area, I don’t think. I mean… work is where it probably shows up the most, because there are emails and notes and things that feel important to get right. But it’s not like I’m only trying to prevent one particular thing from going wrong.\n\nIt’s more that once I start feeling unsure, I try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already handled it. But then one concern leads to the next, and I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo… I’d say it’s more general than specific, if that answers it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are something you feel forced to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… though “forced” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t feel like I’m being made to do it exactly, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked or that it’s probably fine. But then another concern comes up, and then another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I really should just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it does feel hard to control… even if I don’t really want to keep doing it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think of them as “not me,” exactly… I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and responsible, and I suppose I value that.

But the repeating part—going back over something when I know, at least logically, that I’ve already done enough—doesn’t feel like how I’d prefer to be. It feels a bit excessive, maybe. I don’t like that I get caught in it.

So… I’d say the carefulness feels like me, but the needing to keep checking doesn’t really fit with how I’d like to handle things. Does that make sense?

But then one concern leads into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose the fear of making a mistake is a big part of it… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or reviews primarily focused on preventing something from going wrong in a specific area of your life?\n\nPatient: Not one specific area, I don’t think. I mean… work is where it probably shows up the most, because there are emails and notes and things that feel important to get right. Yes, I think so… at least partly. It’s not that I enjoy doing it, obviously. It’s more that when the worry starts, I feel uncomfortable leaving it alone.

I’ve had stressful periods, of course—work and life and decisions piling up—but I don’t think there’s one particular event that stands out in that way.\n\nI hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more general and about everything in life?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, there are a few themes that come up a lot—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing. And then I start wondering what I’m going to be worth, I suppose, once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know some of that is normal to think about, but my mind does tend to circle around it… probably more than it needs to. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything. It’s more that I slow things down. With the retirement handoff, I keep second-guessing decisions and then postponing parts of it, telling myself I just need to review things once more.\n\nAnd at the clinic, I can lose time going back over things, which makes the day feel less manageable. Even the things I usually enjoy, like painting or gardening, I’ll put off because they feel like more than I can quite take on at the moment. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance, but… yes, probably some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts over and over?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… not in a ritual sort of way, if that’s what you mean. More that I’ll try to talk myself through it logically, the way I would with a nervous patient, and for a moment it helps.\n\nBut then one concern seems to lead straight into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same questions more than once. I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I suppose… but I do have trouble letting it go. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to perform these repetitive behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there is an urge, though I’d hesitate to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more like I feel I can’t quite settle until I’ve done something about the thought.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and that helps briefly. But then one concern leads to the next, and I end up checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same question more than once. I know that probably isn’t necessary, but in the moment it feels hard to just leave it alone. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I’m not thinking something terrible will happen in a very specific sense, but I do feel like if I don’t address the worry, I’m being careless.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and that helps for a bit. But then one concern leads to another, and I end up checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question again when I probably don’t need to. I tell myself I’m just being responsible, but… I think I’m also trying to stop something from going wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like repeating these behaviors or mental acts helps to reduce your anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… briefly, I think it does. I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m not trying to be difficult or anything, but when I check the email again or go back over my notes, I do feel a little calmer for a moment.\n\nThe problem is it doesn’t seem to last very long. I’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and I can almost settle it… but then another concern comes up, and then I’m reviewing something again or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go. So yes, it reduces the worry a bit, but not in a way that really holds.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no… at least not that I can name clearly. It’s more that I feel uneasy if I don’t follow the thought through.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, the way I might with a nervous patient, and sometimes that helps for a minute. But then another concern pops up, and I’m back checking an email, or looking over notes again, or asking the same question when I probably already know the answer.\n\nSo I suppose I’m trying to prevent mistakes in a general sense, but it’s not usually one exact disaster I’m picturing. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like making a mistake is one of the main things that's driving your need to repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m incapable or anything, but making a mistake is definitely part of what gets me caught up.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, almost the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked it or that it’s probably fine. But then one concern leads into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose the fear of making a mistake is a big part of it… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or reviews primarily focused on preventing something from going wrong in a specific area of your life?\n\nPatient: Not one specific area, I don’t think. I mean… work is where it probably shows up the most, because there are emails and notes and things that feel important to get right. But it’s not like I’m only trying to prevent one particular thing from going wrong.\n\nIt’s more that once I start feeling unsure, I try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already handled it. But then one concern leads to the next, and I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo… I’d say it’s more general than specific, if that answers it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are something you feel forced to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… though “forced” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t feel like I’m being made to do it exactly, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked or that it’s probably fine. But then another concern comes up, and then another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I really should just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it does feel hard to control… even if I don’t really want to keep doing it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive checks or reviews are in line with how you want to be, or do they feel like they're not really you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think of them as “not me,” exactly… I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and responsible, and I suppose I value that.\n\nBut the repeating part—going back over something when I know, at least logically, that I’ve already done enough—doesn’t feel like how I’d prefer to be. It feels a bit excessive, maybe. I don’t like that I get caught in it.\n\nSo… I’d say the carefulness feels like me, but the needing to keep checking doesn’t really fit with how I’d like to handle things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are aimed at relieving anxiety or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least partly. It’s not that I enjoy doing it, obviously. It’s more that when the worry starts, I feel uncomfortable leaving it alone.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself through it, almost like I would with a nervous patient — “You’ve already handled this, it’s fine” — but then another concern comes up, and then another. And before I know it I’m checking emails again, looking back over notes, or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it is to settle myself down a bit… though it doesn’t always work for very long. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

And before I know it I’m checking emails again, looking back over notes, or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it is to settle myself down a bit… though it doesn’t always work for very long. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your worries or repetitive behaviors been interfering with your daily life, relationships, or work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 64 / dentist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve had to slow down and postpone parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions and losing time at the clinic, and even my painting and gardening don’t feel as manageable as they used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes… I mean, I’m still functioning, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it has started to get in the way.

With the retirement handoff, I’ve had to slow things down and postpone parts of it because I keep second-guessing decisions, and I lose time at the clinic going back over things. It’s frustrating, because I’d like to handle it more smoothly.

And even at home, things I usually enjoy—painting, gardening—don’t feel quite as manageable as they used to. Not impossible, just… harder to settle into. So yes, I suppose it is interfering some.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally made me make the appointment is… my concentration has gotten a bit unreliable. I mean, I can still do my work, of course, but I’ll be reviewing a chart at the clinic, or even doing something I enjoy like laying out a watercolor wash, and then I realize I’ve read the same line three times. My mind has wandered off to all the things that could go wrong. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you mean, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about the future or things that could go wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because some worry is normal, I know. But my mind does keep circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd then there’s this… concern that once I’m fully retired, I’ll become sort of useless. It feels a bit out of proportion even to me, honestly. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, with it suddenly peaking within a few minutes. I get worked up, certainly, but it’s more of a steady worrying than an abrupt wave of fear. At least, that’s how I’d put it—if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty most of the time… no, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I mean, I have my moments, like anyone, and I can get discouraged with myself, but I wouldn’t say it’s a constant sadness. At least, not most of the time. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no… at least nothing I’d call traumatic. I’ve had stressful periods, of course—work and life and decisions piling up—but I don’t think there’s one particular event that stands out in that way.\n\nI hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more general and about everything in life?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, there are a few themes that come up a lot—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing. And then I start wondering what I’m going to be worth, I suppose, once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know some of that is normal to think about, but my mind does tend to circle around it… probably more than it needs to. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything. It’s more that I slow things down. With the retirement handoff, I keep second-guessing decisions and then postponing parts of it, telling myself I just need to review things once more.\n\nAnd at the clinic, I can lose time going back over things, which makes the day feel less manageable. Even the things I usually enjoy, like painting or gardening, I’ll put off because they feel like more than I can quite take on at the moment. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance, but… yes, probably some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts over and over?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… not in a ritual sort of way, if that’s what you mean. More that I’ll try to talk myself through it logically, the way I would with a nervous patient, and for a moment it helps.\n\nBut then one concern seems to lead straight into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same questions more than once. I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I suppose… but I do have trouble letting it go. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to perform these repetitive behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there is an urge, though I’d hesitate to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more like I feel I can’t quite settle until I’ve done something about the thought.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and that helps briefly. But then one concern leads to the next, and I end up checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same question more than once. I know that probably isn’t necessary, but in the moment it feels hard to just leave it alone. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I’m not thinking something terrible will happen in a very specific sense, but I do feel like if I don’t address the worry, I’m being careless.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and that helps for a bit. But then one concern leads to another, and I end up checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question again when I probably don’t need to. I tell myself I’m just being responsible, but… I think I’m also trying to stop something from going wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like repeating these behaviors or mental acts helps to reduce your anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… briefly, I think it does. I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m not trying to be difficult or anything, but when I check the email again or go back over my notes, I do feel a little calmer for a moment.\n\nThe problem is it doesn’t seem to last very long. I’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and I can almost settle it… but then another concern comes up, and then I’m reviewing something again or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go. So yes, it reduces the worry a bit, but not in a way that really holds.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no… at least not that I can name clearly. It’s more that I feel uneasy if I don’t follow the thought through.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, the way I might with a nervous patient, and sometimes that helps for a minute. But then another concern pops up, and I’m back checking an email, or looking over notes again, or asking the same question when I probably already know the answer.\n\nSo I suppose I’m trying to prevent mistakes in a general sense, but it’s not usually one exact disaster I’m picturing. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like making a mistake is one of the main things that's driving your need to repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m incapable or anything, but making a mistake is definitely part of what gets me caught up.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, almost the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked it or that it’s probably fine. But then one concern leads into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose the fear of making a mistake is a big part of it… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or reviews primarily focused on preventing something from going wrong in a specific area of your life?\n\nPatient: Not one specific area, I don’t think. I mean… work is where it probably shows up the most, because there are emails and notes and things that feel important to get right. But it’s not like I’m only trying to prevent one particular thing from going wrong.\n\nIt’s more that once I start feeling unsure, I try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already handled it. But then one concern leads to the next, and I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo… I’d say it’s more general than specific, if that answers it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are something you feel forced to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… though “forced” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t feel like I’m being made to do it exactly, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked or that it’s probably fine. But then another concern comes up, and then another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I really should just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it does feel hard to control… even if I don’t really want to keep doing it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive checks or reviews are in line with how you want to be, or do they feel like they're not really you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think of them as “not me,” exactly… I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and responsible, and I suppose I value that.\n\nBut the repeating part—going back over something when I know, at least logically, that I’ve already done enough—doesn’t feel like how I’d prefer to be. It feels a bit excessive, maybe. I don’t like that I get caught in it.\n\nSo… I’d say the carefulness feels like me, but the needing to keep checking doesn’t really fit with how I’d like to handle things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are aimed at relieving anxiety or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least partly. It’s not that I enjoy doing it, obviously. It’s more that when the worry starts, I feel uncomfortable leaving it alone.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself through it, almost like I would with a nervous patient — “You’ve already handled this, it’s fine” — but then another concern comes up, and then another. name: Uncontrollable_Worry\n  description: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.\n  manifestation: I try to reason with myself the way I would with a nervous patient, but one concern just leads to the next until I’m checking emails, reviewing notes, or asking the same questions again instead of letting it go.\n- key: S059\n  name: Concentration_Difficulty\n  description: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.\n  manifestation: What finally got me here is that my concentration has gotten unreliable—I’ll be reviewing a chart at the clinic or laying out a watercolor wash, and suddenly I realize I’ve read the same line three times while my mind has wandered to everything that could go wrong.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve had to slow down and postpone parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions and losing time at the clinic, and even my painting and gardening don’t feel as manageable as they used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since I started the formal retirement process about a year and three months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 64 / dentist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S059\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive checks or reviews are in line with how you want to be, or do they feel like they're not really you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think of them as “not me,” exactly… I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and responsible, and I suppose I value that.\n\nBut the repeating part—going back over something when I know, at least logically, that I’ve already done enough—doesn’t feel like how I’d prefer to be. It feels a bit excessive, maybe. I don’t like that I get caught in it.\n\nSo… I’d say the carefulness feels like me, but the needing to keep checking doesn’t really fit with how I’d like to handle things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are aimed at relieving anxiety or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least partly. It’s not that I enjoy doing it, obviously. It’s more that when the worry starts, I feel uncomfortable leaving it alone.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself through it, almost like I would with a nervous patient — “You’ve already handled this, it’s fine” — but then another concern comes up, and then another. And before I know it I’m checking emails again, looking back over notes, or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it is to settle myself down a bit… though it doesn’t always work for very long. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or repetitive behaviors been interfering with your daily life, relationships, or work?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still functioning, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it has started to get in the way.\n\nWith the retirement handoff, I’ve had to slow things down and postpone parts of it because I keep second-guessing decisions, and I lose time at the clinic going back over things. It’s frustrating, because I’d like to handle it more smoothly.\n\nAnd even at home, things I usually enjoy—painting, gardening—don’t feel quite as manageable as they used to. Not impossible, just… harder to settle into. So yes, I suppose it is interfering some.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your worrying or repetitive behaviors are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Yes… I suppose it is causing me distress, though I’m a little reluctant to make it sound dramatic.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally made me make the appointment is… my concentration has gotten a bit unreliable. I mean, I can still do my work, of course, but I’ll be reviewing a chart at the clinic, or even doing something I enjoy like laying out a watercolor wash, and then I realize I’ve read the same line three times. My mind has wandered off to all the things that could go wrong. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you mean, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about the future or things that could go wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because some worry is normal, I know. But my mind does keep circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd then there’s this… concern that once I’m fully retired, I’ll become sort of useless. It feels a bit out of proportion even to me, honestly. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, with it suddenly peaking within a few minutes. I get worked up, certainly, but it’s more of a steady worrying than an abrupt wave of fear. At least, that’s how I’d put it—if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty most of the time… no, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I mean, I have my moments, like anyone, and I can get discouraged with myself, but I wouldn’t say it’s a constant sadness. At least, not most of the time. I’ve had stressful periods, of course—work and life and decisions piling up—but I don’t think there’s one particular event that stands out in that way.\n\nI hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more general and about everything in life?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, there are a few themes that come up a lot—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing. And then I start wondering what I’m going to be worth, I suppose, once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know some of that is normal to think about, but my mind does tend to circle around it… probably more than it needs to. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything. It’s more that I slow things down. With the retirement handoff, I keep second-guessing decisions and then postponing parts of it, telling myself I just need to review things once more.\n\nAnd at the clinic, I can lose time going back over things, which makes the day feel less manageable. Even the things I usually enjoy, like painting or gardening, I’ll put off because they feel like more than I can quite take on at the moment. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance, but… yes, probably some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts over and over?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… not in a ritual sort of way, if that’s what you mean. More that I’ll try to talk myself through it logically, the way I would with a nervous patient, and for a moment it helps.\n\nBut then one concern seems to lead straight into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same questions more than once. I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I suppose… but I do have trouble letting it go. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to perform these repetitive behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there is an urge, though I’d hesitate to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more like I feel I can’t quite settle until I’ve done something about the thought.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and that helps briefly. But then one concern leads to the next, and I end up checking emails again, or reviewing notes I’ve already looked at, or asking the same question more than once. I know that probably isn’t necessary, but in the moment it feels hard to just leave it alone. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I’m not thinking something terrible will happen in a very specific sense, but I do feel like if I don’t address the worry, I’m being careless.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and that helps for a bit. But then one concern leads to another, and I end up checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question again when I probably don’t need to. I tell myself I’m just being responsible, but… I think I’m also trying to stop something from going wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like repeating these behaviors or mental acts helps to reduce your anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… briefly, I think it does. I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m not trying to be difficult or anything, but when I check the email again or go back over my notes, I do feel a little calmer for a moment.\n\nThe problem is it doesn’t seem to last very long. I’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and I can almost settle it… but then another concern comes up, and then I’m reviewing something again or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go. So yes, it reduces the worry a bit, but not in a way that really holds.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no… at least not that I can name clearly. It’s more that I feel uneasy if I don’t follow the thought through.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, the way I might with a nervous patient, and sometimes that helps for a minute. But then another concern pops up, and I’m back checking an email, or looking over notes again, or asking the same question when I probably already know the answer.\n\nSo I suppose I’m trying to prevent mistakes in a general sense, but it’s not usually one exact disaster I’m picturing. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like making a mistake is one of the main things that's driving your need to repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m incapable or anything, but making a mistake is definitely part of what gets me caught up.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, almost the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked it or that it’s probably fine. But then one concern leads into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose the fear of making a mistake is a big part of it… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or reviews primarily focused on preventing something from going wrong in a specific area of your life?\n\nPatient: Not one specific area, I don’t think. I mean… work is where it probably shows up the most, because there are emails and notes and things that feel important to get right. But it’s not like I’m only trying to prevent one particular thing from going wrong.\n\nIt’s more that once I start feeling unsure, I try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already handled it. But then one concern leads to the next, and I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo… I’d say it’s more general than specific, if that answers it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are something you feel forced to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… though “forced” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t feel like I’m being made to do it exactly, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked or that it’s probably fine. But then another concern comes up, and then another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I really should just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it does feel hard to control… even if I don’t really want to keep doing it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive checks or reviews are in line with how you want to be, or do they feel like they're not really you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think of them as “not me,” exactly… I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and responsible, and I suppose I value that.\n\nBut the repeating part—going back over something when I know, at least logically, that I’ve already done enough—doesn’t feel like how I’d prefer to be. It feels a bit excessive, maybe. I don’t like that I get caught in it.\n\nSo… I’d say the carefulness feels like me, but the needing to keep checking doesn’t really fit with how I’d like to handle things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are aimed at relieving anxiety or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least partly. It’s not that I enjoy doing it, obviously. It’s more that when the worry starts, I feel uncomfortable leaving it alone.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself through it, almost like I would with a nervous patient — “You’ve already handled this, it’s fine” — but then another concern comes up, and then another. I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m not trying to be difficult or anything, but when I check the email again or go back over my notes, I do feel a little calmer for a moment.\n\nThe problem is it doesn’t seem to last very long. I’ll try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and I can almost settle it… but then another concern comes up, and then I’m reviewing something again or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go. So yes, it reduces the worry a bit, but not in a way that really holds.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no… at least not that I can name clearly. It’s more that I feel uneasy if I don’t follow the thought through.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, the way I might with a nervous patient, and sometimes that helps for a minute. But then another concern pops up, and I’m back checking an email, or looking over notes again, or asking the same question when I probably already know the answer.\n\nSo I suppose I’m trying to prevent mistakes in a general sense, but it’s not usually one exact disaster I’m picturing. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like making a mistake is one of the main things that's driving your need to repeat these behaviors or mental acts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m incapable or anything, but making a mistake is definitely part of what gets me caught up.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, almost the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked it or that it’s probably fine. But then one concern leads into another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes I’ve already gone over, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose the fear of making a mistake is a big part of it… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or reviews primarily focused on preventing something from going wrong in a specific area of your life?\n\nPatient: Not one specific area, I don’t think. I mean… work is where it probably shows up the most, because there are emails and notes and things that feel important to get right. But it’s not like I’m only trying to prevent one particular thing from going wrong.\n\nIt’s more that once I start feeling unsure, I try to reason with myself, the way I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already handled it. But then one concern leads to the next, and I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I should probably just let it go.\n\nSo… I’d say it’s more general than specific, if that answers it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are something you feel forced to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… though “forced” sounds a little strong, maybe. I don’t feel like I’m being made to do it exactly, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\nI’ll try to reason with myself, like I would with a nervous patient, and tell myself I’ve already checked or that it’s probably fine. But then another concern comes up, and then another, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, reviewing notes, or asking the same question when I really should just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it does feel hard to control… even if I don’t really want to keep doing it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive checks or reviews are in line with how you want to be, or do they feel like they're not really you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think of them as “not me,” exactly… I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and responsible, and I suppose I value that.\n\nBut the repeating part—going back over something when I know, at least logically, that I’ve already done enough—doesn’t feel like how I’d prefer to be. It feels a bit excessive, maybe. I don’t like that I get caught in it.\n\nSo… I’d say the carefulness feels like me, but the needing to keep checking doesn’t really fit with how I’d like to handle things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors or mental acts are aimed at relieving anxiety or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least partly. It’s not that I enjoy doing it, obviously. It’s more that when the worry starts, I feel uncomfortable leaving it alone.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself through it, almost like I would with a nervous patient — “You’ve already handled this, it’s fine” — but then another concern comes up, and then another. And before I know it I’m checking emails again, looking back over notes, or asking the same question when I probably should just let it go.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it is to settle myself down a bit… though it doesn’t always work for very long. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or repetitive behaviors been interfering with your daily life, relationships, or work?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still functioning, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it has started to get in the way.\n\nWith the retirement handoff, I’ve had to slow things down and postpone parts of it because I keep second-guessing decisions, and I lose time at the clinic going back over things. It’s frustrating, because I’d like to handle it more smoothly.\n\nAnd even at home, things I usually enjoy—painting, gardening—don’t feel quite as manageable as they used to. Not impossible, just… harder to settle into. So yes, I suppose it is interfering some.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying or repetitive behaviors are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is causing me distress, though I’m a little reluctant to make it sound dramatic.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things — whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sensible, whether I’ll end up feeling useless once I’m fully retired. And even as I’m saying it, part of me knows the level of worry is probably more than the situation calls for.\n\nIt’s upsetting, honestly. Not constant panic or anything like that, but it wears on me. I don’t feel as settled in my own mind as I’d like to. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
